Sherrington strapped a dog into a harness and pinched one of the dog’s feet.","After a fraction of a second, the dog flexed (raised) the pinched leg and extended the other legs.","Sherrington found the same reflexive movements after he made a cut that disconnected the spinal cord from the brain. In an intact animal, messages descending from the brain modify the reflexes, making them stronger at some times and weaker at others.","When Sherrington pinched a dog’s foot, the dog flexed that leg after a short delay.","During the delay, an impulse had to travel up an axon from the skin receptor to the spinal cord, and then an impulse had to travel back down the leg to a muscle.","Sherrington measured the distance that the impulse traveled from skin to spinal cord to muscle, and then he calculated the speed at which the impulse traveled. In contrast, previous research had measured action potential velocities along sensory or motor nerves at about 40 m/s.","Sherrington concluded that some process must be slowing conduction through the reflex, and he inferred that the delay occurs where one neuron communicates with another (Figure 2.2).","The neuron delivering transmission is the presynaptic neuron, and the one receiving it is the postsynaptic neuron.","Sherrington proposed that although a single subthreshold excitation in the postsynaptic neuron decays over time, it combines with a second excitation that follows quickly.","With a rapid succession of pinches, each adds its effect to what remained from the previous ones, until the combination exceeds the threshold of the postsynaptic neuron, producing an action potential.","Decades later, Sherrington’s former student, John Eccles, attached microelectrodes to stimulate axons of presynaptic neurons while he recorded from the A graded depolarization is an excitatory postsynaptic potential (EPSP), which results from a sodium ions entering the neuron.","When Eccles produced two EPSPs within a short time, the second EPSP added to what was left of the first one (point 2 in Figure 2.3), producing temporal summation.","Sherrington also found that synapses have the property of spatial summation —summation over space.","This time, instead of pinching one point twice, he pinched two points at once.","Sherrington concluded that pinching two points activated sensory neurons whose axons converged","Excitation from either sensory axon excited that spinal neuron, and a combination of excitations exceeded the threshold to produce an action potential (point 4 in Figure 2.3). Again, Eccles confirmed Sherrington’s inference, demonstrating that EPSPs from several axons summate their effects on a postsynaptic cell (Figure 2.4).","Temporal summation and spatial summation occur together when a neuron receives input from several axons in succession.","A dog raising one leg needs to extend the other legs to maintain balance.","The dog also relaxed the extensor muscles of the stimulated leg and the flexor muscles of the other legs.","The interneuron also sends messages to inhibit the extensor muscles in that leg and the flexor muscles of the three other legs. Most neurons have a spontaneous firing rate, a periodic production of action potentials even without synaptic input.","In such cases, the EPSPs increase the frequency of action potentials above the spontaneous rate, whereas IPSPs decrease it.","For a neuron with a spontaneous firing rate of 10 action potentials per second, a stream of EPSPs might increase the rate to 15 or more, whereas a preponderance of IPSPs might decrease it to 5 or fewer. among neurons, but for a given neuron, it stays within a narrow range.","Sherrington’s work opened the way to exploring the wiring diagram of the nervous system.","When neuron 1 excites neuron 3, it also excites neuron 2, which inhibits neuron 3.","The excitatory message reaches neuron 3 faster because it goes through just one synapse instead of two.","The result is a burst of excitation (EPSP) in neuron 3, which quickly slows or stops. The axon from either cell A or cell B stimulates cell X with 1 1 unit.","If the threshold of cell X is 1 1, then cell X responds to “A or B.” If the threshold is 1 2, then cell X responds to","The EPSPs and IPSPs reaching a neuron at a given moment compete with one another.","The combined effect of EPSPs and IPSPs determines whether or not the postsynaptic cell fires an action potential.","However, do not imagine that any single neuron decides what you will eat for breakfast. However, some synapses produce fast, brief effects, and others produce slow, long-lasting effects.","The effect of two synapses at the same time can be more than double the effect of either one, or less than double.","Also, many neurons in the brain change their properties from one context to another. Synapse","kind":"detailed_source_grounded_notes","printedStart":48,"printedEnd":64,"pdfStart":66,"pdfEnd":82,"content":[{"type":"paragraph","text":"Chemical transmission proceeds through neurotransmitter synthesis, storage, release, receptor action, and eventual inactivation or reuptake. Different receptor mechanisms produce effects with different timing and duration."},{"type":"paragraph","text":"The module contrasts chemical and electrical synapses and relates synaptic mechanisms to drugs and hormones. Study the sequence as a cycle, noting where the effects of a transmitter can be increased, decreased, or stopped."},{"type":"heading","id":"bp14-m2-2-study-1","text":"A chemical signal in stages"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Chemical transmission begins when a neuron synthesizes or obtains neurotransmitter molecules and stores them in vesicles. Arrival of an action potential at the terminal changes membrane voltage and opens calcium channels; calcium entry triggers vesicle fusion and transmitter release. Molecules diffuse across the synaptic cleft and bind to receptors on the receiving cell. Their effects depend strongly on receptor type. Transmission ends or changes when transmitter molecules diffuse away, are broken down, or are taken back up by cells. Keep these stages separate because drugs and diseases can affect different steps."},{"type":"heading","id":"bp14-m2-2-study-2","text":"Receptors are as important as transmitters"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"The same transmitter does not always have the same effect. Ionotropic receptors directly control ion channels and commonly produce relatively rapid responses. Metabotropic receptors act through intracellular signaling systems and may have slower, longer-lasting effects. A chemical’s action depends on which receptors and pathways are present in a particular tissue. This is why a simple list of 'excitatory neurotransmitters' and 'inhibitory neurotransmitters' cannot explain every synapse. A receptor can amplify, change, or suppress a response."},{"type":"heading","id":"bp14-m2-2-study-3","text":"Regulation, feedback, and competing routes"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Presynaptic and postsynaptic processes help regulate how much transmitter is released and how cells respond. Reuptake transporters, enzymes, and feedback receptors can prevent chemical signaling from continuing indefinitely. Some cells communicate through electrical synapses, where current passes more directly through specialized connections, allowing particularly rapid coordination. Hormones differ from ordinary point-to-point synaptic signals because they can travel through the bloodstream and act on distant targets, although the nervous and endocrine systems interact."},{"type":"heading","id":"bp14-m2-2-study-4","text":"Why this matters for behavior and medication"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"A drug might increase transmitter availability, block a receptor, mimic a transmitter, alter release, or change breakdown. These interventions can lead to very different outcomes even when they all concern one neurotransmitter system. The right question is not simply 'Which chemical causes this behavior?' but 'Which neurons, receptors, and signaling steps participate, and under what conditions?' This pathway-based view will recur in the chapters on movement, sleep, mood, and disorders."},{"type":"heading","id":"deep-bp14-m2-2-h01","text":"The Discovery of Chemical Transmission at Arrival of an action potential at the terminal changes membrane voltage and opens calcium channels; calcium entry triggers vesicle fusion and transmitter release. Molecules diffuse across the synaptic cleft and bind to receptors on the receiving cell. Their effects depend strongly on receptor type. Transmission ends or changes when transmitter molecules diffuse away, are broken down, or are taken back up by cells. After feeling discouraged all day, he awoke the following night at 3 a.m. with the same idea, rushed to the laboratory, and performed the experiment.","Despite Loewi’s work, most researchers over the next three decades continued to believe that most synapses were electrical and that chemical synapses were the exception.","Finally, in the 1950s, researchers established that chemical transmission predominates throughout the nervous system. A small number of electrical synapses do exist, however, as discussed later in this module.","Understanding the chemical events at a synapse is fundamental to understanding the nervous system.","As we do, we also consider drugs that affect certain steps in this process.","Most members of the animal kingdom have all or nearly all the same transmitters that humans have. Ctenophores (Figure 2.11), possibly representative of the earliest, most primitive animals, apparently have only one neurotransmitter, glutamate.","Glutamate acts even in the plant kingdom, transmitting messages to help the plant react to a damaged part.","The oddest transmitter is nitric oxide (chemical formula NO), a gas released by many small local neurons. (Do not confuse nitric oxide, NO, with nitrous oxide, N 2 O, sometimes known as “laughing gas.”) Nitric oxide is toxic in large quantities and difficult to make in a laboratory.","In addition to influencing other neurons, nitric oxide dilates the nearby blood vessels, thereby increasing blood flow to that brain area. Neurons synthesize nearly all neurotransmitters from amino acids, which the body obtains from proteins in the diet.","Most members of the animal kingdom have all or nearly all the same transmitters that humans have.","Ctenophores (Figure 2.11), possibly representative of the earliest, most primitive animals, apparently have only one neurotransmitter, glutamate.","Glutamate acts even in the plant kingdom, transmitting messages to help the plant react to a damaged part. The oddest transmitter is nitric oxide (chemical formula NO), a gas released by many small local neurons. (Do not confuse nitric oxide, NO, with nitrous oxide, N 2 O, sometimes known as “laughing gas.”) Nitric oxide is toxic in large quantities and difficult to make in a laboratory.","In addition to influencing other neurons, nitric oxide dilates the nearby blood vessels, thereby increasing blood flow to that brain area.","Neurons synthesize nearly all neurotransmitters from amino acids, which the body obtains from proteins in the diet.","Note the relationship among epinephrine, norepinephrine, and dopamine—compounds known as catecholamines, because they contain a catechol group and an amine group.","Fewer than one in a thousand neurons release serotonin, but those neurons have axons that branch widely in the nervous system, producing important psychological effects. The amino acid tryptophan, the precursor to serotonin, crosses the blood–brain barrier by a special transport system that it shares with other large amino acids.","However, tryptophan competes with more abundant amino acids, including phenylalanine, that share the same transport system, so eating more protein is not a reliable way to add tryptophan to the brain.","One way to increase tryptophan in the brain is to decrease consumption of phenylalanine. It has no therapeutic use, but researchers sometimes use it to study the functions of dopamine.","Chemical synapses usually keep a supply of neurotransmitter ready in the presynaptic terminal. Most transmitters are synthesized near their release site, and many are packed into small membrane-bound vesicles. Storing the molecules close to the synapse lets a neuron release them when the appropriate signal arrives.","Vesicles are tiny, nearly spherical packets in the presynaptic terminal. They hold a high concentration of neurotransmitter molecules until the cell releases their contents into the synaptic cleft.","The textbook also describes a pool of neurotransmitter outside these packets in the terminal. Storage and the chemical supply of a transmitter are therefore related but are not identical processes.","An action potential by itself does not release the neurotransmitter at the end of the axon.","The calcium entry causes exocytosis —a burst of release of neurotransmitter from the presynaptic neuron.","The released neurotransmitter diffuses across the synaptic cleft to the postsynaptic membrane, where it attaches to a receptor.","The neurotransmitter takes no more than 0.01 ms to diffuse across the cleft, which is only 20 to 30 nanometers (nm) wide. For many years, investigators believed that each neuron released just one neurotransmitter, but later researchers found that many, probably most, neurons release a combination of two or more transmitters.","simultaneously release an excitatory transmitter and an inhibitory transmitter, the equivalent of saying “yes” and “no” at the same time.","In contrast to the voltage-gated channels along an axon, the channels controlled by a neurotransmitter are transmitter-gated or ligand-gated. (A ligand is a chemical that binds to something.) When the neurotransmitter attaches, it opens a channel.","Whereas most ionotropic effects depend on either glutamate or GABA, metabotropic synapses use many chemicals, including dopamine, norepinephrine, and serotonin, neuropeptides... and sometimes glutamate and GABA too.","However, a cluster of related axons might fire a burst of action potentials, releasing a cloud of the neuromodulator that diffuses to many receptors.","Metabotropic synapses are suited for more enduring effects such as taste, smell, and pain, where the exact timing is less important. Metabotropic effects are also important for arousal, attention, hunger, thirst, and emotion—functions that arise slowly and last longer than a sensation.","Because different receptors control different functions, drugs can have specialized effects on behavior.","A given receptor can have different effects for different people, or even in different parts of one person’s brain, because of differences in the hundreds of proteins that surround synapses, tether the presynaptic neuron to the postsynaptic neuron, and guide neurotransmitter molecules to their receptors. Soon investigators found that the brain produces its own neuropeptides, now known as endorphins (a contraction of endo genous m orphines ), that bind to the same receptors as opiates.","This discovery was important because it indicated that opiates relieve pain by acting on receptors in the brain as well as in the skin.","One is recycling, so that the presynaptic neuron has transmitters available for release again.","The choline diffuses back to the presynaptic neuron, which reattaches it to form acetylcholine again.","Although reuptake is efficient, it takes time, and the presynaptic neuron does not reabsorb every molecule it releases.","The reuptake process varies among individuals and from one brain area to another, but it is slower than reuptake of glutamate or GABA. In many cases the slow reuptake is a benefit, because it enables the chemicals to build up enough concentration to have significant effects on their receptors.","Because resynthesizing these large molecules takes time, a neuron can temporarily exhaust its supply.","Stimulant drugs, including amphetamine and cocaine, inhibit the transporters for dopamine, serotonin, and norepinephrine, decreasing reuptake and prolonging the effects of the neurotransmitters.","Most antidepressant drugs also block reuptake of one or more neurotransmitters, but more weakly than amphetamine and cocaine do. Methylphenidate blocks the reuptake of dopamine in the same way that cocaine does, at the same brain receptors.","A methadone pill increases arousal at first, but a few hours later the user may experience reduced energy and motivation.","Don’t send it again.” A couple of mechanisms in the nervous system serve that function.","First, many presynaptic terminals have receptors sensitive to the same transmitter they release.","These receptors are known as autoreceptors —receptors that respond to the released transmitter by inhibiting further synthesis and release. Second, some postsynaptic neurons respond to stimulation by releasing chemicals that travel back to the presynaptic terminal to inhibit further release of transmitter.","Two reverse transmitters are anandamide (from the Sanskrit word anana, meaning “bliss”) and 2-AG ( sn -2 arachidonylglycerol).","Cannabinoids, the active chemicals in marijuana, mimic the effects of these reverse transmitters, telling a presynaptic neuron, “I received your message.","Stop sending it.” The presynaptic cell, unaware that it hadn’t sent a message, cancels whatever it had been about to send. In this way, the chemicals in marijuana decrease the release of both glutamate and GABA.","Because electrical transmission is faster than even the fastest chemical transmission, electrical synapses have evolved in cases where exact synchrony between cells is important.","For example, some of the cells that control your breathing are synchronized by electrical synapses. (It’s important","to inhale on the left side at the same time as on the right side.) Many animal species have electrical synapses in the system responsible for coordinating rapid escape movements. Large pores of the membrane of one neuron line up precisely with pores in the membrane of the other cell.","Whenever one of the neurons is depolarized, sodium ions from that cell pass immediately into the other neuron and depolarize it, too.","Hormonal influences resemble synaptic transmission in many ways, and many chemicals serve as both hormones and neurotransmitters.","A hormone is a chemical secreted by cells in one part of the body and conveyed by the blood to influence other cells.","Hormones function more like radio waves: They convey a message to any receiver tuned to the right station.","The body releases tiny amounts of some chemicals that might or might not act as hormones.) Just as circulating hormones modify brain activity, hormones secreted by the brain control the secretion of other hormones. The hypothalamus also synthesizes releasing hormones and inhibiting hormones that control the anterior pituitary’s release of","For example, when the level of thyroid hormone is low, the hypothalamus releases TSH-releasing hormone, which stimulates the anterior pituitary to release TSH, which in turn causes the thyroid gland to secrete more thyroid hormones (Figure 2.21).","In the time since Sherrington, we have come a long way in our understanding of synapses.","Drugs can modify them in many ways, for good or bad, and so can experiences.","Understanding how the nervous system produces our behavior and experiences is largely a matter of understanding synapses.","They’re also presented in alphabetical order with definitions in the book’s Subject Index/Glossary at the end of this book. We want to know whether B is just an extension of axon A or whether A formed an excitatory synapse on some neuron in the ganglion, whose axon is axon B.","However, if dopamine axons were destroyed, amphetamine and cocaine would lose their effects.","Nitric oxide differs from the usual vesicle-storage pattern. The spinal cord, autonomic nervous system, hindbrain, midbrain, and forebrain participate in different but interconnected functions."},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Build a mental map of the major structures rather than memorizing names without context. Pay particular attention to how the thalamus, hypothalamus, basal ganglia, hippocampus, and ventricles fit into the larger organization."},{"type":"heading","id":"bp14-m3-1-study-1","text":"The basic map"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"The central nervous system includes the brain and spinal cord; the peripheral nervous system includes structures outside them. Sensory routes carry information toward central processing, while motor routes influence muscles and organs. Anatomical words such as anterior, posterior, dorsal, ventral, medial, and lateral specify location relative to the body or nervous system. Learn these directions before memorizing structures: an accurate map helps prevent mixing up nearby names that have very different functions."},{"type":"heading","id":"bp14-m3-1-study-2","text":"Spinal cord, autonomic system, and brainstem"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"The spinal cord carries signals between body and brain and participates in reflexes. The autonomic nervous system regulates functions such as heart activity, digestion, and glandular secretion. Its sympathetic and parasympathetic divisions often support different bodily demands but are not simply permanent 'on' and 'off' systems. In the hindbrain, structures including the medulla, pons, and cerebellum support vital regulation and coordinated activity. The midbrain participates in sensory processing and movement-related functions."},{"type":"heading","id":"bp14-m3-1-study-3","text":"Forebrain structures worth distinguishing"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"The thalamus is a major relay and processing hub for many sensory pathways. The hypothalamus helps regulate basic bodily needs and works closely with the pituitary gland. Basal ganglia contribute to selection and control of actions; the basal forebrain has broad influences on cortical function. The hippocampus is especially important to memory, while the cerebral cortex supports many aspects of perception, cognition, and action. The ventricles are fluid-filled cavities connected to the circulation of cerebrospinal fluid. These descriptions are functional signposts, not one-function-per-structure definitions."},{"type":"heading","id":"bp14-m3-1-study-4","text":"How to study anatomy effectively"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Study pathways instead of memorizing only lists. For example, ask where a sensory signal enters the spinal cord, which central structures process it, and how an action command reaches a muscle. For any structure, identify where it sits, major connections, and the kind of evidence linking it to a function. Anatomical labels become meaningful when they explain why injury at different locations has different effects."},{"type":"heading","id":"deep-bp14-m3-1-h01","text":"Terminology to Describe the Nervous The module closes by encouraging students to revisit these structures as they appear again in later chapters.","paragraphIndex":20058,"bullets":[{"label":"Start with the overall organization","explanation":"Distinguish the central nervous system from the peripheral nervous system, and locate the spinal cord, hindbrain, midbrain, and forebrain within that broader organization.","paragraphIndex":20059,"printedPages":[79]},{"label":"Connect structures to functions","explanation":"Review how spinal pathways carry sensory and motor information; how the sympathetic and parasympathetic divisions organize bodily responses; and how the thalamus, hypothalamus, basal ganglia, and hippocampus contribute to behavior.","paragraphIndex":20060,"printedPages":[79]},{"label":"Use repeated review","explanation":"The textbook explicitly recommends returning to the anatomy module as new structures recur. Revisiting locations alongside their behavioral functions is more useful than treating a list of names as isolated facts.","paragraphIndex":20062,"printedPages":[78]}],"status":"editorial_source_checked_classroom_note","basis":"uploaded_textbook_pdf_and_markdown","evidenceMethod":"PDF_body_page_manual_rewrite","sourceFactCount":4,"editorialVerification":"individually_paraphrased_against_source_PDF"}],"classroomParagraphs":["For vertebrates, we distinguish the central nervous system from the peripheral nervous system (Figure 3.1).","peripheral nervous system (PNS) connects the brain and spinal cord to the rest of the body.","Part of the PNS is the somatic nervous system, which consists of the axons conveying messages from the sense organs to the CNS and from the CNS to the muscles. Another part of the PNS, the autonomic nervous system, controls the body’s organs.","A special part of the autonomic nervous system is the enteric nervous system, which controls the digestive system.","Because the nervous system is threedimensional, we need more terms to describe it.","However, when humans evolved upright posture, the position of the head changed relative to the spinal cord. Consequently, the dorsal–ventral axis of the human brain is at a right angle to the dorsal–ventral axis of the spinal cord.","The spinal cord communicates with all the sense organs and muscles except those of the head.","The cell bodies of the sensory neurons are in clusters of neurons outside the spinal cord, called the dorsal root ganglia. ( Ganglia is the plural of ganglion, a cluster of neurons.","In most cases, a neuron cluster outside the CNS is called a ganglion, and a cluster inside the CNS is called a nucleus.) Cell bodies of the motor neurons are inside the spinal cord.","Many neurons from the gray matter of the spinal cord send axons to the brain or to other parts of the spinal cord through the white matter, containing myelinated axons. Each segment of the spinal cord sends sensory information to the brain and receives motor commands from the brain.","If the spinal cord is cut at a given segment, the brain loses sensation from that segment and below.","The autonomic nervous system consists of neurons that receive information from and send commands to the heart, intestines, and other organs.","Because the sympathetic ganglia are closely linked, they often act as a single system “in sympathy” with one another, although certain events activate some parts more than others.","The parasympathetic nervous system, sometimes called the “rest and digest” system, facilitates vegetative, nonemergency responses. For example, the sympathetic nervous system increases heart rate, and the parasympathetic nervous system decreases it.","The parasympathetic nervous system increases digestive activity, whereas the sympathetic nervous system decreases it.","Unlike the ganglia in the sympathetic system, the parasympathetic ganglia are not arranged in a chain near the spinal cord.","Parasympathetic activity decreases heart rate, increases digestive rate, and, in general, conserves energy. Most sympathetic nervous system axons release norepinephrine, although a few, such as those onto the sweat glands, use acetylcholine.","Because the two systems use different transmitters, certain drugs excite or inhibit one system or the other. Because the flow of sinus fluids is a parasympathetic response, drugs that block the parasympathetic system inhibit sinus flow.","Certain decongestant pills containing pseudoephedrine have been withdrawn or restricted because of their potential for abuse.","Some neuroscientists prefer terms with Greek roots: rhombencephalon (hindbrain), mesencephalon (midbrain), and prosencephalon (forebrain).","The hindbrain, the posterior part of the brain, consists of the medulla, the pons, and the cerebellum.","The medulla and pons, the midbrain, and certain central structures of the forebrain constitute the brainstem (Figure 3.8).","In elephants, the trigeminal nerve that receives sensations from the face is huge because it innervates the trunk, a highly sensitive structure for elephants. Because opiate receptors, which suppress activity, are abundant in the medulla, opiates can produce a dangerous decrease in breathing and heart rate.","The cerebellum is a large hindbrain structure with many deep folds.","It is one of the first brain areas to start developing and one of the last to complete development. The cerebellum has long been known for its contributions to the control of movement, and many older textbooks describe the cerebellum as important for “balance and coordination.” True, people with cerebellar damage are clumsy and lose their balance, but the functions of the cerebellum extend far beyond balance and coordination.","One hypothesis is that the cerebellum’s primary function is to detect sequences of events.","As the name implies, the midbrain is between the hindbrain and the forebrain, although in adult mammals the forebrain surrounds it.","The roof of the midbrain is called the tectum. ( Tectum is the Latin word for “roof.” The same root occurs in the geological term plate tectonics.) The swellings on each side of the tectum are the superior colliculus and the inferior colliculus (Figures 3.8 and 3.10). Under the tectum lies the tegmentum, the intermediate level of the midbrain. (In Latin, tegmentum means a “covering,” such as a rug on the floor.","midbrain structures.) Another midbrain structure, the substantia nigra, gives rise to a dopamine-containing pathway that facilitates readiness for movement.","The forebrain, the most prominent brain area for mammals and birds, consists of two cerebral hemispheres, left and right (Figure 3.11).","Each hemisphere is organized to receive sensory information, mostly from the contralateral (opposite) side of the body.","It controls muscles, mostly on the contralateral side, by way of axons to the spinal cord and the cranial nerve nuclei.","The thalamus is the main source of input to the cortex. The hypothalamus is essential for control of eating, drinking, temperature control, and reproductive behaviors.","The amygdala is part of a circuit that is central for evaluating emotional information, especially with regard to fear.","The thalamus is a pair of structures (left and right) in the center of the forebrain.","Most sensory information goes first to the thalamus, which processes it and sends output to the cerebral cortex.","An exception to this rule is olfactory information, which goes from the olfactory receptors to the olfactory bulbs and then directly to the cerebral cortex.","Many nuclei of the thalamus receive their input from a sensory system, such as vision, process it in various ways, and transmit information to an area of the cerebral cortex, as in Figure 3.14. Another function of the thalamus relates to memory.","When you hold information in working memory, activity bounces back and forth between the frontal cortex and the thalamus.","The hypothalamus, a small area just ventral to the thalamus (Figures 3.10 and 3.12), has widespread connections with the rest of the brain.","Damage in the hypothalamus leads to abnormalities in motivated behaviors, such as feeding, drinking, temperature regulation, sexual behavior, or fighting. Because of these important behavioral effects, the small hypothalamus attracts much research attention.","Partly through nerves and partly by releasing hormones, the hypothalamus conveys messages to the pituitary gland, altering its release of hormones (Figure 3.10).","The basal ganglia, a group of subcortical structures lateral to the thalamus, include the caudate nucleus, the putamen,","Many authorities include other structures as well, especially the nucleus accumbens.","It has long been known that damage to the basal ganglia impairs movement, as in conditions such as Parkinson’s disease and Huntington’s disease.","The basal ganglia integrate motivational and emotional behavior to increase the vigor of selected actions. However, the role of the basal ganglia extends beyond movement.","They are critical for gradual learning of skills and habits.","A structure on the ventral surface of the forebrain, the nucleus basalis, receives input from the hypothalamus and basal ganglia and sends axons that release acetylcholine to widespread areas in the cerebral cortex (Figure 3.16).","The nucleus basalis is a key system for arousal, wakefulness, and attention.","Patients with Parkinson’s disease and Alzheimer’s disease have impairments of attention and intellect because of inactivity or deterioration in the nucleus basalis.","The hippocampus is a forebrain structure closely associated with memory and orientation in space. Kalat introduces its role here and examines the evidence in greater detail in the later memory chapter. At this stage, understand the two functions that link it to everyday behavior: remembering particular experiences and keeping track of location.","The hippocampus is especially important for memory for individual events. This is different from saying that every kind of memory is stored entirely in one brain structure.","It also helps monitor where you are and where you are going. Remembering an experience and navigating through an environment both require information about relations among places and events.","The canal persists into adulthood as the central canal in the center of the spinal cord, and as the ventricles, four fluid-filled cavities within the brain.","Cells called the choroid plexus along the walls of the four ventricles produce cerebrospinal fluid (CSF), a clear fluid similar to blood plasma.","CSF flows from the lateral ventricles to the third and fourth ventricles, and then into the central canal of the spinal cord and the narrow spaces between the brain and the meninges, membranes that surround the brain and spinal cord.","Although the brain has no pain receptors, the meninges do, and meningitis—inflammation of the meninges— is painful. It also provides a reservoir of hormones and nutrition for the brain and spinal cord.","If the flow of CSF is obstructed, it accumulates in the ventricles or in the space between brain and meninges, increasing pressure on the brain.","Neuroanatomy becomes easier when each structure is connected to its location, its larger system, and its function. The module closes by encouraging students to revisit these structures as they appear again in later chapters.","Distinguish the central nervous system from the peripheral nervous system, and locate the spinal cord, hindbrain, midbrain, and forebrain within that broader organization.","Review how spinal pathways carry sensory and motor information; how the sympathetic and parasympathetic divisions organize bodily responses; and how the thalamus, hypothalamus, basal ganglia, and hippocampus contribute to behavior.","This is an anatomical introduction, not a complete account of hippocampal memory. Module 12.2 expands the discussion of hippocampal damage and other memory-related brain areas.","The textbook explicitly recommends returning to the anatomy module as new structures recur. Revisiting locations alongside their behavioral functions is more useful than treating a list of names as isolated
